I actually got sent to the hallway quite a bit because I couldn't sit still or wait my turn, even though I was smart and did well on tests.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention and restlessness problems continue through your teens and adulthood, or did they mostly go away until the recent work change?\n\nPatient: They never truly went away, but they were much easier to manage when I could stay active and organize my work around my own rhythms. I managed to cope through my teens and earlier career, but the recent restructuring and all these long, sitting meetings have really brought these lifelong struggles to a head.\n\nDoctor: When you were younger and even now, do you still often act before thinking—for example interrupting people, blurting out answers, or having trouble waiting your turn—more than just feeling inattentive or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Even back in elementary school, my report cards always mentioned me talking over classmates and not being able to wait my turn. Today, I still struggle with it constantly—I'll blurt out answers in meetings before people even finish their questions, or jump into coworkers' conversations and finish their sentences for them without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do these impulsive or hyperactive behaviors cause problems in more than one setting, such as at work and at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely affect both areas. At work, my restlessness and zoning out lead to embarrassing mistakes in negotiations and force me to work late to fix errors. At home, it's starting to strain my relationship because I'm too distracted and restless to just sit and relax with my partner in the evenings."

them while my mind is off thinking about what to make for dinner, so I can’t repeat back what they just said.\n- S010 (Fidgeting): I’m constantly jiggling my leg, clicking my pen, or twisting my ring in meetings—even when I tell myself to stop, a minute later I’m tapping the table again without realizing it and people glance over at me.\n- S011 (Leaving_Seat): During those two‑hour budget reviews, I’ll jump out of my skin.\n- S014 (Constant_Activity): My colleagues joke that I can’t sit still because even on quieter days I’m pacing between my desk and the warehouse, starting one task, then another, then reorganizing something at home in the evening instead of ever just truly resting.\n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings I’ll cut people off and answer before they’ve finished explaining the question, because as soon as I think I know where they’re going I blurt out my response and then realize I missed an important nuance at the end.\n- S018 (Interrupting_Intruding): I catch myself jumping into other people’s conversations in the office or finishing their sentences and redirecting the discussion, and only afterward do I notice the looks that say I’ve overstepped or derailed their train of thought.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in elementary school, teachers would write on my report cards that I was always out of my seat, talking over classmates, and getting sent into the hallway because I couldn’t sit still or wait my turn, even though I was smart and did well on tests.\n- functional_impairment: Since taking on this leadership role, my restlessness and zoning out in long meetings have led to embarrassing mistakes in negotiations and extra hours fixing avoidable errors, and it’s starting to strain my relationship because I’m often distracted and can’t just sit and relax with my partner in the evenings.\n- duration: These problems have really come to a head over the last couple of years since the I also tend to blurt out answers and interrupt people, which has been a struggle since elementary school when teachers always complained about me being out of my seat and talking over others.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your main problem is being physically restless and impulsive, or is it mostly trouble paying attention and staying organized without much hyperactivity?"

I keep popping up from my chair under the pretense of refilling my water or grabbing a printout, because sitting there the whole time feels almost unbearable to me.\n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): Any another, then reorganizing something at home in the evening instead of ever just truly resting.\n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings I’ll cut people off and answer before they’ve finished explaining the question, because as soon as I think I know where they’re going I blurt out my response and then realize I missed an important nuance at the end.\n- S018 (Interrupting_Intruding): I I actually got sent to the hallway quite a bit because I couldn't sit still or wait my turn, even though I was smart and did well on tests.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention and restlessness problems continue through your teens and adulthood, or did they mostly go away until the recent work change?\n\nPatient: They never truly went away, but they were much easier to manage when I could stay active and organize my work around my own rhythms. At home, it's starting to strain my relationship because I'm too distracted and restless to just sit and relax with my partner in the evenings.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a long history of being physically very restless and impulsive, like fidgeting, leaving your seat, interrupting, or acting before thinking, not just trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have a long history of that—I'm constantly fidgeting, jiggling my leg, and I often have to get up during meetings because sitting still gives me a wired, crawling feeling inside. I also tend to blurt out answers and interrupt people, which has been a struggle since elementary school when teachers always complained about me being out of my seat and talking over others.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your main problem is being physically restless and impulsive, or is it mostly trouble paying attention and staying organized without much hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: I actually experience a combination of both, but the physical restlessness and impulsivity are extremely prominent and hard to control. If I have to sit still, I get a wired, crawling feeling inside that forces me to fidget, pace, or impulsively blurt things out and interrupt others. While I definitely zone out and miss details, that physical urge to always be in motion is a massive, unavoidable part of my daily struggle.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, were your main problems mostly being unable to pay attention, or were they equally marked by being very physically restless and impulsive, like constantly fidgeting, leaving your seat, and interrupting others?"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes, absolutely.
